CMTO Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Effleurage performed as a smooth gliding stroke): effleurage is the gliding stroke used to introduce oil, warm tissue, and assess the area before deeper work begins. It is also used to close a treatment because it has a calming, sedative quality. Petrissage involves lifting and squeezing tissue rather than gliding over it. Tapotement and friction are far more stimulating and localized, so neither fits the description of a smooth continuous stroke.
  2. The correct answer is D (Petrissage, involving lifting and kneading of soft tissue): petrissage lifts, squeezes, and rolls muscle tissue to mobilize fluid and reduce adhesions within the muscle belly. Effleurage stays superficial and glides rather than lifting tissue. Vibration uses fine trembling movements rather than kneading. Tapotement strikes the tissue rhythmically instead of squeezing it.
  3. The correct answer is B (Tapotement, a family of rhythmic percussive strokes): hacking, cupping, clapping, and pincement are all forms of tapotement, which uses brisk alternating hand contacts to stimulate circulation and muscle tone. Petrissage lifts and kneads tissue rather than striking it rhythmically. Effleurage is a slow gliding stroke, the opposite of a percussive movement. Joint mobilization addresses joint capsules, not superficial soft tissue percussion.
  4. The correct answer is B (Mobilize scar tissue and reduce fiber cross-linking): transverse friction is applied perpendicular to the fibers to break up disorganized collagen cross-links and encourage a more mobile, functional scar. It is a localized and often uncomfortable technique, not a relaxing whole-body approach. It targets a specific lesion rather than promoting broad lymphatic flow. It also does not lengthen the entire muscle the way a sustained stretch would.
  5. The correct answer is A (Vibration, delivered as fine oscillatory shaking): vibration is a fine, trembling oscillation transmitted through the hand into the tissue, often used over nerve pathways or to help loosen secretions. Petrissage lifts and kneads rather than trembling in place. Effleurage glides smoothly across the skin instead of oscillating stationary. Friction is a small circular or transverse pressure movement over a specific lesion, not a trembling motion.
  6. The correct answer is B (Assessing tissue tone and calming the nervous system): light superficial effleurage is used to introduce touch, assess surface tissue quality, and elicit a relaxation response before deeper techniques begin. Breaking down fibrous adhesions requires sustained deeper pressure such as friction, not a light stroke. Effleurage does not permanently lengthen muscle fibers on its own. Joint capsule mobilization is a separate manual technique entirely distinct from a gliding skin stroke.
  7. The correct answer is C (Improved venous and lymphatic return with reduced tension): the rhythmic compression and release of petrissage assists venous and lymphatic flow while reducing muscular tension and adhesions in the tissue. It increases rather than decreases local blood flow and temperature. It does not permanently alter tendon collagen structure through a single application. It also does not meaningfully suppress nerve conduction velocity in healthy tissue.
  8. The correct answer is D (A client with an unstable cardiac arrhythmia): vigorous percussive strokes over the chest can stress the cardiovascular system, making them unsuitable when a client has an unstable arrhythmia or other cardiac instability. Healthy athletes preparing for activity are actually a common appropriate population for stimulating tapotement. General circulatory stimulation and pre-event activation are both accepted goals for tapotement in stable clients, not contraindications.
  9. The correct answer is A (It separates adhered fibers and mobilizes the local lesion): applying friction across rather than along the fiber direction physically separates fibers that have become adhered, breaking up the disorganized scar tissue at the lesion site. It is a stimulating, often uncomfortable technique, not a sedative one. It targets a localized lesion rather than uniformly stretching the whole muscle-tendon unit. Any skin warming is incidental, not the primary rationale for the perpendicular direction.
  10. The correct answer is D (A sustained, low-load stretch applied to fascia to release restriction): myofascial release uses gentle, sustained pressure and stretch held over time to allow the viscoelastic fascia to elongate and restrictions to soften. It is not a brisk tapping technique, which describes tapotement instead. It is also not a rapid trembling motion, which describes vibration. It relies on sustained low-load stretch rather than a quick high-velocity thrust.
  11. The correct answer is C (A hyperirritable nodule within a taut band that refers pain): a trigger point is a discrete, hyperirritable spot located within a taut band of skeletal muscle that is painful on compression and can refer pain to a distant area. It is a focal nodule, not simply a diffuse area of tenderness across the whole muscle. It is a muscular phenomenon rather than a joint capsule restriction. It also has nothing to do with a skin lesion produced by friction technique.
  12. The correct answer is A (The amplitude and location of movement within available range): Maitland grades I through V describe how large the oscillation is and whether it occurs within the available range or pushes into and beyond resistance and end-range. Duration in minutes is not what defines the grade of the technique. The grading system applies to joint movement generally, not to one specific muscle group. Tissue temperature beforehand also plays no role in how the grades are defined.
  13. The correct answer is D (A pumping action from alternating vasodilation and vasoconstriction): alternating hot and cold cycles cause blood vessels to dilate and constrict repeatedly, creating a pumping effect that enhances local circulation and helps clear metabolic waste. A single sustained vasodilation would only occur with continuous heat, not alternating temperatures. Contrast bathing is not meant to produce prolonged numbness like sustained ice application. It is also more stimulating than sedating, unlike a plain warm soak.
  14. The correct answer is C (The amount and source of force moving the limb): these categories are distinguished by how much force the client generates independently versus how much comes from the therapist, gravity, or external resistance. The specific end joint angle is a range of motion measurement, not the defining feature of the exercise category. Tissue temperature and repetition count may be documented but do not define which category the movement falls into.
  15. The correct answer is C (A slow, sustained hold at the end of available range): static stretching involves moving a muscle slowly to the end of its available range and holding that position without bouncing. A rapid bouncing motion instead describes ballistic stretching, a different and higher-risk approach. Rhythmic tapping describes tapotement, a manual massage stroke rather than a stretch. A quick reflexive contraction followed by release more closely resembles a PNF technique, not static stretching.
  16. The correct answer is B (Autogenic inhibition following an isometric muscle contraction): PNF techniques use an isometric contraction of the target muscle to trigger Golgi tendon organ activity, producing autogenic inhibition that allows a deeper stretch afterward. The technique does not work by mechanically tearing collagen fibers, which would be an injury rather than a therapeutic effect. It does not fully block nerve conduction to the muscle. Vasoconstriction of the arterial supply is not the mechanism responsible for the increased stretch tolerance.
  17. The correct answer is D (It follows the natural direction of venous and lymphatic flow): centripetal effleurage strokes are directed toward the heart because this matches the natural direction of venous return and lymphatic drainage, supporting circulation. It is not chosen simply to shorten treatment time. Reducing therapist hand fatigue is not the physiological rationale behind the stroke direction. Direction toward the heart does not by itself make deeper pressure safer to apply.
  18. The correct answer is B (One hand compresses tissue while the other lifts and releases): classic kneading petrissage alternates compression from one hand with a lifting and releasing motion from the other, creating a rolling, squeezing rhythm through the muscle belly. Both hands striking together describes a tapotement pattern, not kneading. Hands remaining still would not produce any kneading action at all. Gliding together in the same direction without lifting describes effleurage rather than petrissage.
  19. The correct answer is C (The ulnar border of loosely relaxed hands with fingers extended and slightly separated): hacking uses the outer, ulnar edge of relaxed hands with extended, slightly separated fingers striking alternately and rapidly against the tissue, producing a light stimulating percussion. Using the flat palm instead describes clapping or slapping, a different tapotement variation. Small circular thumb pressure describes a friction technique, not tapotement. The heel of the hand driving into deep tissue more closely resembles a compression or kneading stroke.
  20. The correct answer is C (A loosening, stimulating percussive vibration through the tissue): the trapped air in the cupped hand creates a hollow percussive sound and a stimulating vibration that can help loosen mucus or tone superficial muscle. It is a rapid rhythmic stroke, not a sustained static compression held over minutes. It does not glide across the skin the way effleurage does for lymphatic drainage. A single percussive stroke also does not permanently change the length of muscle fibers.
  21. The correct answer is D (A more organized, mobile scar with improved fiber alignment): repeated friction sessions are thought to stimulate a controlled inflammatory response that remodels disorganized scar tissue into a more aligned, mobile structure over time. Tendon tissue is not replaced by muscle fibers through friction. Collagen is a normal component of healthy tendon and is not meant to be eliminated entirely. Friction targets the tendon lesion itself, not the surrounding joint capsule's laxity.
  22. The correct answer is A (The amplitude and speed of the oscillation produced): fine vibration uses small, rapid, low-amplitude trembling, while coarse vibration uses larger, slower, higher-amplitude shaking through the same body part. The lubricant color has no relevance to how the technique is classified. Vibration is applied as a stationary oscillation rather than as a directional stroke across the fibers. The number of fingers used does not define whether vibration is fine or coarse.
  23. The correct answer is D (Direct technique applies force into the barrier of restriction): direct myofascial release engages and sustains pressure directly into the restrictive barrier until it softens, whereas indirect technique follows the tissue toward ease away from the barrier. Direct technique does not use rapid percussive strokes, which would describe tapotement instead. It requires sustained contact with the fascia rather than avoiding it. It is a soft tissue fascial approach, not a joint capsule mobilization technique.
  24. The correct answer is A (Local ischemia followed by reactive hyperemia to release the point): sustained compression temporarily restricts blood flow to the trigger point, and the reactive hyperemia that follows release is believed to help flush metabolic byproducts and reduce the nodule's irritability. The taut band is not severed by compression, and there is no cutting involved. Skin temperature changes are incidental, not the primary intended effect. This technique targets a focal muscular point rather than producing joint capsule laxity.
  25. The correct answer is B (A small-amplitude oscillation performed at the very start of range): grade I mobilizations are small oscillations performed at the beginning of the available range, often used for pain relief before any resistance is encountered. A large oscillation moving into resistance instead describes a higher grade such as grade III. A sustained static hold at end range is not how oscillatory grades are defined. A rapid thrust beyond the anatomical limit falls outside the passive oscillation grading system entirely and is not part of the massage therapy scope.
  26. The correct answer is D (Grade IV is a small oscillation performed into resistance): grade IV uses a small-amplitude movement performed at the limit of range, into tissue resistance, while grade II uses a larger oscillation within the free range before resistance is met. Grade IV is not restricted to lower limb joints only and can be applied anywhere mobilization is appropriate. Oscillation speed is not what differentiates these two grades. A thorough assessment of joint range is still expected before applying grade IV, not skipped.
  27. The correct answer is B (Vasoconstriction that helps limit local swelling and pain): cold application constricts local blood vessels, which helps control swelling, slow nerve conduction of pain signals, and reduce metabolic demand right after an acute injury. Ice does not warm tissue, so it does not promote healing through warming. It does not lengthen ligamentous tissue, since cold reduces rather than increases tissue extensibility. The numbing effect from ice is temporary, not a permanent change to the nerve endings.
  28. The correct answer is C (Increased tissue extensibility through vasodilation and warming): moist heat dilates local blood vessels and warms the muscle, which increases tissue extensibility and prepares it for stretching or deeper manual work. Heat increases rather than reduces local blood flow. It can make trigger points easier to treat but does not eliminate them on its own. It also does not permanently lengthen tendon collagen without an accompanying stretch being performed.
  29. The correct answer is B (The client's own muscle contraction moves the joint independently): in active range of motion, the client generates the muscular force needed to move the joint through its available range without outside assistance. A therapist moving the limb without client effort instead describes passive range of motion. A mechanical device driving the joint describes a different assisted modality, not active movement. Gravity lowering a limb passively also does not require active muscle contraction from the client.
  30. The correct answer is C (A client unable to actively move a joint without assistance): passive range of motion is used when the client cannot generate the movement independently, so the therapist or an external force moves the joint through its available range. Building maximal strength requires active muscular effort, which passive movement does not provide. A client who can fully resist movement is better suited to active or resisted exercise. High-intensity resisted strengthening also depends on active client effort, not passive movement.
  31. The correct answer is B (An external force opposing the client's active muscle contraction): resisted range of motion adds manual or mechanical resistance against the client's own active contraction, increasing the demand on the muscle. Having the therapist perform the entire movement describes passive rather than resisted exercise. A static stretch at end range is a separate technique, not a defining feature of resisted movement. Complete muscle relaxation throughout the movement is the opposite of what resisted exercise requires from the client.
  32. The correct answer is A (It can trigger a protective stretch reflex that resists lengthening): the rapid bouncing motion of ballistic stretching can activate the muscle spindle stretch reflex, causing a reflexive contraction that resists lengthening and raises injury risk. It does not produce a stronger reactive hyperemia than other stretching approaches. It is not more reliably effective at increasing collagen elasticity than a controlled static hold. Ballistic stretching is also a stimulating rather than a sedative parasympathetic-dominant technique.
  33. The correct answer is C (Slow, rhythmic effleurage and gentle petrissage at moderate pressure): slow rhythmic strokes at a comfortable pressure favor a parasympathetic, relaxing response consistent with a stress reduction goal. Aggressive friction is a localized, often uncomfortable technique aimed at a specific lesion, not general relaxation. Vigorous tapotement is stimulating rather than calming. Rapid resisted exercise is physically demanding and better suited to a strengthening goal than to relaxation.
  34. The correct answer is D (Sustained deep pressure strokes targeting the deeper muscle layer): reaching deep, longstanding tension requires sustained pressure applied progressively into the deeper tissue layer rather than a light or brief technique. Light superficial effleurage stays at the surface and will not access deeper tension. Brief tapotement is too short and too stimulating to address longstanding deep tension. Passive range of motion without pressure mobilizes the joint rather than releasing tension held within the muscle tissue.
  35. The correct answer is A (An extremely light, feather-like effleurage stroke over the skin): a nerve stroke is an extremely light, superficial gliding stroke, often applied with just the fingertips, used to signal the end of a sequence or the whole treatment. A deep sustained compression is the opposite of the light quality that defines a nerve stroke. A rapid percussive stroke describes tapotement, not this gentle finishing stroke. A vigorous friction stroke across the fibers is a deep, stimulating technique unrelated to the nerve stroke's light quality.
  36. The correct answer is B (The superficial fascia and its adherence to underlying tissue): skin rolling lifts and moves the skin and superficial fascia to assess and release restrictions between that layer and the tissue beneath it. It does not directly access the deep joint capsule or ligamentous structures, which lie much deeper. It is a superficial technique, not one aimed at the deepest muscle layer near bone. It also has no direct effect on vertebral discs or nerve roots, which are well beyond the reach of superficial skin rolling.
  37. The correct answer is A (Cupping uses hollowed hands while clapping uses flat hands): the defining difference is hand shape, with cupping using a hollowed, cup-like hand to trap air and clapping using a flatter hand surface for a sharper contact. Both strokes typically alternate between the two hands, so that is not the distinguishing feature. Neither stroke is restricted to only the face or only the back by definition. The presence or absence of lubricant is not what defines the difference between these two strokes.
  38. The correct answer is D (Perpendicular, or transverse, to the direction of the fibers): effective cross-fiber friction is applied transverse to the fiber direction so the strokes actively separate and mobilize adhered fibers within the lesion. A parallel stroke would glide along the fibers rather than mobilizing across them, reducing its effectiveness. A fixed forty-five degree diagonal is not the standard convention taught for this technique. A figure-eight pattern applied without regard to fiber orientation would not reliably achieve the intended cross-fiber effect.
  39. The correct answer is C (Assisting the loosening and movement of retained secretions): vibration applied over the chest wall can help loosen and mobilize retained respiratory secretions, supporting clearance of the airway. It does not permanently increase total lung capacity through a single application of manual vibration. It also does not directly strengthen the diaphragm, since vibration is a passive technique rather than an active exercise. Vibration does not produce anesthesia of the treated area.
  40. The correct answer is C (Myofascial release using a sustained fascial stretch and hold): sustaining a slow cross-hand stretch and waiting for the tissue to soften describes the classic engage-and-wait approach used in myofascial release. Rapid alternating tapotement is a fast percussive technique, not a slow sustained stretch. Deep transverse friction targets a focal lesion with small perpendicular strokes rather than a broad sustained fascial stretch. Resisted range of motion requires active muscular effort against resistance, which is not part of this passive fascial technique.
  41. The correct answer is A (The temple, angle of the jaw, and posterior neck): upper trapezius trigger points are well documented to refer pain toward the temple, along the side of the face, and into the posterior neck region. Referral to the distal forearm and hand is more characteristic of trigger points in different muscles such as the scalenes or forearm extensors. Anterior chest wall referral over the sternum is more typical of pectoral muscle trigger points. Referral into the lateral lower leg and ankle is unrelated to the upper trapezius and would suggest a different source muscle.
  42. The correct answer is B (A large-amplitude oscillation performed within the free range): grade II mobilizations use a larger oscillation that stays within the available, resistance-free range of the joint. A small oscillation confined to end range instead describes grade IV. A completely static hold without oscillation does not match the oscillatory nature that defines the Maitland grades. A high-velocity thrust past the anatomical barrier falls outside the passive oscillatory grading system entirely.
  43. The correct answer is D (Grade III moves into resistance at the limit of range): grade III uses a large-amplitude oscillation that extends into the resistance encountered near the limit of available range, unlike grade II which stays within the free range. It is not restricted to non-weight-bearing joints only. There is no requirement that the joint be anesthetized before a grade III mobilization is performed. Oscillation speed is not the defining difference between grades II and III.
  44. The correct answer is A (Alternating hot and cold cycles, usually ending in cold): contrast bathing alternates several cycles of hot and cold immersion, commonly finishing on cold to help limit residual swelling. Using cold water alone for the whole session would not create the alternating pumping effect that defines contrast therapy. Using hot water alone throughout also removes the alternating vascular response that is the point of the protocol. A single brief lukewarm dip does not provide the temperature contrast needed to produce the vascular pumping effect.
  45. The correct answer is A (Agitation of the water that provides a mild massaging action): whirlpool units agitate the water, which adds a gentle massaging and circulatory stimulating effect on top of the temperature benefits of immersion. Whirlpool units do include temperature control, so the absence of any control is inaccurate. Ice cubes are not a standard component of a whirlpool unit's design. The limb is often moved actively or passively within a whirlpool rather than being required to stay completely still.
  46. The correct answer is B (Roughly fifteen to thirty seconds per repetition): commonly cited guidelines suggest holding a static stretch for approximately fifteen to thirty seconds to allow the tissue to accommodate and lengthen. Less than one second is too brief for the tissue to adapt and would not qualify as a sustained static hold. A rapid bounce describes ballistic stretching rather than a static hold. Several hours held continuously is impractical and not part of standard static stretching guidelines used in remedial exercise.
  47. The correct answer is C (The client relaxes and the muscle is passively stretched further): after the isometric contraction, the client relaxes the muscle, and the resulting autogenic inhibition allows the therapist to move the limb into a deeper passive stretch. Applying rapid tapotement is not part of the contract-relax sequence. Immersing the limb in ice water afterward is unrelated to this stretching technique. A grade V thrust falls outside massage therapy scope and is not part of the contract-relax stretching sequence.
  48. The correct answer is B (Pressure gradually increases from light to progressively deeper): effleurage is typically graded from light introductory pressure toward deeper strokes as the tissue warms and the therapist assesses tolerance. A steadily decreasing pressure pattern does not match the usual progression used to prepare tissue for deeper work. Completely uniform pressure throughout would not allow the gradual warming and assessment that grading provides. Applying pressure with no consistent pattern would be inconsistent with the deliberate, graded approach taught for effleurage sequencing.
  49. The correct answer is C (The hands move in opposite directions, twisting the tissue): wringing lifts the muscle belly and moves the hands in opposite directions across its width, creating a twisting, kneading action. Pressing straight downward without lateral movement would not create the twisting quality that defines wringing. Using only one hand while the other rests does not match the two-handed opposing action required for this technique. A rapid alternating tap describes tapotement, not the kneading, twisting motion of wringing.
  50. The correct answer is B (Large, well-padded muscle groups such as the gluteals): beating is a firmer percussive stroke best suited to large, well-cushioned muscle areas that can tolerate the added force without discomfort. Applying it directly over bony prominences like the spinous processes would be uncomfortable and is generally avoided. Thin skin over the eye socket is far too delicate and inappropriate for any tapotement variation. An acute unhealed fracture site is a contraindication for percussive techniques of any kind, not an appropriate target area.
  51. The correct answer is A (A brief, controlled hyperemia and mild inflammatory response): friction is thought to create a small, controlled inflammatory and hyperemic response that stimulates the remodeling of disorganized scar tissue into a more mobile structure. The technique specifically aims to change local blood flow rather than avoid any change at all. Encouraging calcification would make the tissue less mobile, which is the opposite of the intended outcome. Friction does not anesthetize the area, and some discomfort during application is expected.
  52. The correct answer is A (Continuous flat or fingertip contact while oscillating in place): vibration is delivered by maintaining continuous contact with the flat hand or fingertips while producing a fine or coarse trembling oscillation, rather than gliding or striking. A single point contact moved continuously across the skin would describe a gliding stroke rather than stationary vibration. A cupped fist used for rapid strikes instead describes a tapotement variation like beating. Vibration also requires actual contact with the skin, unlike a hovering, non-contact technique.
  53. The correct answer is D (The superficial fascial layer and its restrictions): the lack of lubricant allows the therapist's hands to grip and drag the skin, engaging the superficial fascia directly to identify and release restrictions in that layer. Vertebral joint capsules lie much deeper and are not directly engaged through a superficial skin traction technique. Deep tendon attachments at the bone are similarly beyond the reach of this superficial approach. Peripheral nerve trunks are not the structure being targeted by this particular fascial technique.
  54. The correct answer is C (An active point spontaneously produces pain without compression): an active trigger point produces spontaneous pain, often with referral, even without direct pressure, while a latent point is only painful when compressed. Active points are not confined only to postural trunk muscles and can occur throughout the body. Latent points typically refer pain less prominently than active points, not more. Active trigger points do generally respond to manual treatment such as ischemic compression, so claiming they never respond is inaccurate.
  55. The correct answer is D (The scope of practice permitted for massage therapy): high-velocity thrust manipulation is generally considered outside the scope of massage therapy practice and is typically reserved for other regulated professions trained specifically in that technique. It is not simply a matter of personal pacing preference on the therapist's part. Client comfort with slower techniques is a separate consideration from whether the technique itself is within scope. Circulatory benefit does not change the fact that thrust manipulation exceeds the techniques taught and permitted within massage therapy training.
  56. The correct answer is A (The rate of cellular metabolism and secondary hypoxic injury): cold slows local cellular metabolism, which is thought to reduce secondary hypoxic tissue damage in the area surrounding an acute injury. It does not meaningfully change the volume of blood within the heart chambers. A local ice application to a limb does not lower the client's overall core body temperature for hours. Cryotherapy is used acutely for swelling control rather than as a method to improve long-term tendon flexibility.
  57. The correct answer is D (An isometric contraction of the target muscle, with no joint movement allowed): hold-relax uses an isometric contraction of the tight target muscle against resistance with no joint movement, while contract-relax uses a concentric (isotonic) contraction of the target muscle through a range of motion before the final stretch. Tapotement is not part of the PNF stretching sequence. Applying a cold pack is unrelated to the PNF mechanism being described here. A grade V thrust falls outside massage scope and has no role in this stretching technique.
  58. The correct answer is B (Lymphatic drainage and reduction of localized fluid congestion): light effleurage strokes directed toward lymph nodes are used to encourage lymphatic flow and help reduce localized fluid congestion in the tissue. This technique does not directly strengthen skeletal muscle, which requires active exercise instead. It also does not remodel deep scar tissue, a goal more associated with sustained friction over multiple sessions. Effleurage has no anesthetic blocking effect on the regional nerve supply.
  59. The correct answer is D (The muscle is lifted away from the bone and squeezed): picking up specifically lifts the muscle belly away from underlying bone and compresses it between the fingers and thumb, distinguishing it from a flatter kneading stroke performed against the bone. Striking the muscle repeatedly with a fist describes a tapotement variation, not petrissage. Rolling only the skin without lifting the muscle instead describes skin rolling, a different petrissage variant. Passively moving a joint through range is unrelated to this soft tissue lifting technique.
  60. The correct answer is C (The muscle spindle and associated stretch reflex pathways): the rapid rhythmic taps of tapotement are believed to stimulate muscle spindles and related reflex pathways, contributing to its toning and stimulating effect on the muscle. It does not act directly on the vertebral disc annulus, which lies well beneath the muscle layer. Synovial fluid within a joint capsule is not the structure responsible for tapotement's reflexive effect. The effect also extends deeper than the epidermis alone, involving neuromuscular structures rather than skin only.
  61. The correct answer is A (Dense, less contractile tissue such as tendons and ligaments): transverse friction is generally best suited to denser, less contractile connective tissue like tendons and ligaments where fiber cross-linking is the primary problem. It is not equally appropriate for every tissue type regardless of density and contractility. Highly contractile muscle belly tissue is generally better addressed with petrissage or trigger point techniques rather than friction alone. Friction is applied with real pressure into deeper tissue, not limited to the superficial epidermal layer.
  62. The correct answer is C (A broader, more generalized relaxing effect over a larger area): coarse vibration, delivered with larger rocking movements of the whole hand, spreads its effect over a wider area and is often used for a more generalized relaxing sensation. Fine fingertip vibration is the more localized and precise technique, which is the opposite of what coarse vibration provides. Both fine and coarse vibration are believed to have real circulatory and neuromuscular effects, so claiming no effect at all is inaccurate. The effect also extends beyond the superficial skin to influence deeper neuromuscular tissue.
  63. The correct answer is C (A slow, sustained multidirectional stretch across the tissue): the cross-hand technique separates the forearms slowly to create a sustained, multidirectional stretch through the fascia while the hands remain fixed on the skin. A rapid high-velocity thrust is inconsistent with the slow, sustained nature of myofascial work. A brisk repeated percussive tap describes tapotement, a completely different technique. Combining cold application with deep friction is unrelated to how the cross-hand myofascial stretch is performed.
  64. The correct answer is A (A palpable rope-like tightening of a group of muscle fibers): a taut band is a palpable, rope-like strand of tense muscle fibers within which a trigger point nodule is typically found. It is not the same as a generalized diffuse swelling spread throughout the whole muscle. A completely relaxed segment would show no palpable tension at all, which contradicts the definition of a taut band. A visible surface bruise is a skin finding, not the deeper palpable muscular tightening being described here.
  65. The correct answer is B (Repeated rhythmic back-and-forth movements over several seconds): oscillatory mobilizations are applied as repeated, rhythmic back-and-forth movements sustained over a period of seconds within the chosen grade of movement. A single extremely slow movement performed only once does not reflect the oscillatory, repeated nature of these techniques. A single very fast movement performed once is closer to a thrust technique, which falls outside the oscillatory grading system. A completely static hold without any repetitive movement would not qualify as an oscillation at all.
  66. The correct answer is C (Roughly three to four minutes hot to one minute cold): a commonly taught contrast protocol uses several cycles of roughly three to four minutes in hot water followed by about one minute in cold water. Equal one-second intervals repeated would be far too brief to produce a meaningful vascular response. A single ten-minute hot period followed only once by thirty seconds of cold does not reflect the repeated cycling that defines contrast therapy. Skipping the cold phase entirely after hours of heat removes the alternating vascular effect that makes contrast bathing effective.
  67. The correct answer is D (The client's own effort combined with therapist or device assistance): active-assisted range of motion combines the client's own muscular effort with additional help from the therapist, a device, or gravity to complete the movement. Relying only on an external mechanical device without any client input instead describes a passive movement. Gravity acting alone with the client fully relaxed also describes a passive movement rather than an active-assisted one. Applying resistance against active contraction describes resisted exercise, a different category altogether.
  68. The correct answer is C (Contraction of an agonist reflexively inhibits its antagonist): reciprocal inhibition uses the principle that contracting an agonist muscle produces reflexive inhibition of its antagonist, allowing the antagonist to relax and stretch more easily. Cold application is unrelated to this specific neurological mechanism and does not disable the antagonist muscle. There is no severance of a nerve supply involved in this normal reflexive process. A general increase in core body temperature is not the mechanism responsible for reciprocal inhibition.
  69. The correct answer is B (It provides a consistent, comfortable transition and closing touch): effleurage bookends a treatment because its gliding, connective quality makes it ideal for introducing touch at the start and for providing a calming transition out of the treatment at the end. It is typically used multiple times throughout a session, not only once. It is also commonly used at the start of treatment, not exclusively at the very end. Effleurage complements rather than replaces the other techniques used during a full treatment.
  70. The correct answer is D (A restriction located in muscle lying close to the bone): compressing tissue against the underlying bone allows the therapist to reach and mobilize muscle that lies close to the skeletal surface, where lifting the tissue away is not practical. This variation reaches deeper than the superficial skin layers alone. It is a soft tissue muscular technique, not one that directly addresses the joint capsule. It also is not confined solely to the tendon sheath, since it targets the muscle belly itself.
  71. The correct answer is D (Delicate or superficial areas such as loose skin folds): pincement is a light, gentle pinching stroke best suited to delicate or superficial tissue such as loose skin folds where firmer percussion would be inappropriate. Deep gluteal muscle mass requiring firm percussion is better suited to a stronger stroke like beating rather than a light pinch. Acute muscle spasm is generally treated cautiously and is not the typical target area for a stimulating pinching stroke. Large weight-bearing joints requiring thrust manipulation fall entirely outside what tapotement, including pincement, is designed to address.
  72. The correct answer is B (To mechanically separate adhered fibers within the local lesion): moving perpendicular to the tendon fibers allows the stroke to actively separate fibers that have become adhered at the site of the lesion, which is the central mechanical goal of the technique. The technique is often mildly uncomfortable by design, not intended to avoid all sensation. It is not meant to lengthen the tendon overall, since that is not how localized cross-fiber work functions. It also has no established effect on permanently broadening the tendon's diameter.
  73. The correct answer is B (A calming, sedative parasympathetic-dominant response): slow, low-amplitude vibration is generally associated with a calming, sedative effect consistent with parasympathetic dominance, unlike faster, more stimulating techniques. This is different from tapotement, which tends to be more sympathetically stimulating rather than calming. Vibration is understood to have a genuine physiological effect, so claiming no detectable response at all is inaccurate. Slow sedative vibration would not be expected to immediately raise heart rate and blood pressure the way a stimulating technique might.
  74. The correct answer is D (A curved gliding stretch that engages and releases a fascial line): the J-stroke traces a curved path resembling the letter J, gliding with sustained pressure to engage and stretch a fascial restriction along its length. It does not involve a rapid percussive tapping motion, which would describe tapotement instead. It also involves gliding movement rather than a purely static compression with no motion. A deep circular friction confined to one fixed point is a different, more localized technique than the sweeping J-shaped glide.
  75. The correct answer is A (The taut band of muscle fibers containing the trigger point): a local twitch response is a brief contraction of the taut band of muscle fibers, often considered a useful sign that the trigger point itself has been engaged. It is not related to a superficial vein being compressed against bone. The dermis contracting in response to skin pressure does not describe the muscular twitch response associated with trigger points. A lymph node responding to compression is unrelated to the local twitch phenomenon observed in trigger point work.
  76. The correct answer is A (The end-feel, describing the sensation at end of range): end-feel refers to the specific quality of resistance the therapist perceives when a joint reaches the limit of its passive range, such as a firm capsular stop or a soft tissue approximation. A referral zone instead describes where pain travels from a trigger point, an unrelated concept. A taut band describes a palpable restriction within a muscle, not a joint end-range sensation. Reactive hyperemia describes a vascular response following compression, unrelated to joint end-feel assessment.
  77. The correct answer is C (Vasoconstriction, opposite to the vasodilation of the hot phase): the cold phase of contrast bathing produces local vasoconstriction, which alternates with the vasodilation produced during the hot phase to create the pumping vascular effect. Claiming the cold phase produces vasodilation identical to the hot phase misses the entire purpose of alternating temperatures. Vessel diameter does measurably change between the two phases, so no change occurring at all is inaccurate. The local vascular effect is not the same as a sustained systemic drop in blood pressure lasting for hours.
  78. The correct answer is D (Muscle tension develops while joint angle remains unchanged): an isometric contraction generates muscular tension without any corresponding change in the joint angle, since the muscle length stays essentially constant. Tension developing while the joint moves through full range instead describes an isotonic contraction. No tension developing during passive joint movement describes passive range of motion rather than an active isometric exercise. A steadily decreasing tension as the joint angle changes also does not match the constant-length nature of an isometric hold.
  79. The correct answer is A (Microtrauma or strain from the rapid bouncing forces involved): the rapid, uncontrolled bouncing forces used in ballistic stretching increase the risk of microtrauma or strain to muscle fibers, especially in cold or unprepared tissue. It does not guarantee a complete muscle rupture every time it is performed, since most instances do not result in a full tear. It is generally associated with an increased injury risk rather than a permanent decrease in joint range of motion on its own. Ballistic stretching also does not cause a lasting drop in local skin temperature.
  80. The correct answer is D (A parasympathetic, calming response favoring relaxation): slow, rhythmic effleurage is chosen for relaxation-focused work because it favors parasympathetic activation, promoting a calm, restful state in the client. Faster strokes are more associated with a sympathetic, alerting response, which is the opposite of what a relaxation-focused treatment aims for. Slow effleurage is not intended to raise respiratory rate and heart rate, since that would work against the calming goal. It also does not produce a numbing effect comparable to a topical anesthetic.
  81. The correct answer is A (Venous and lymphatic return traveling back toward the heart): proximally directed petrissage strokes support venous and lymphatic return by encouraging fluid movement back toward the heart along its natural pathway. Arterial delivery into distal capillaries moves in the opposite direction and is not what a proximally directed stroke is designed to assist. Petrissage does not meaningfully alter nerve conduction velocity along peripheral nerves. It also has no direct effect on synovial fluid production within a joint capsule.
  82. The correct answer is A (A recently diagnosed unstable cardiac condition or arrhythmia): percussive strokes applied near the chest can place additional stress on the cardiovascular system, making them inappropriate for a client with an unstable cardiac condition or arrhythmia. Mild seasonal allergies without cardiac or respiratory involvement would not typically require avoiding tapotement over the chest. A minor bruise on the opposite limb has no bearing on whether chest percussion is safe. General daily fatigue alone, without other red flags, is not a specific contraindication to this technique.
  83. The correct answer is B (Firm, consistent contact without slipping over the skin surface): effective friction requires the therapist's fingers to move the underlying tissue together with the skin, maintaining firm, non-slipping contact rather than gliding over the surface. Loose, intermittent contact that keeps lifting off the skin would prevent the sustained pressure friction depends on. Friction is typically performed without oil precisely so the skin does not slip under the fingers. Using the fingernail tips rather than the finger pads would risk discomfort and reduce control rather than support effective technique.
  84. The correct answer is B (Warm tissue with effleurage, apply focused friction, then calm with slower strokes): combining goals typically means warming tissue first with effleurage, addressing the adhesion with focused friction once tissue is prepared, then returning to slower strokes to leave the client relaxed. Using only tapotement throughout would neither prepare tissue properly nor leave the client in a relaxed state. Applying friction first without warming the tissue increases discomfort and risk rather than supporting sound technique selection. Relying only on passive range of motion would not address the localized muscular adhesion the treatment plan specifically calls for.
  85. The correct answer is A (To stimulate circulation and tone through rapid rhythmic percussion): hacking is a percussive tapotement stroke meant to stimulate blood flow and muscle tone through fast alternating contact. It does not stretch fascia along one direction, which describes friction work. It also does not address joint capsule restriction, which requires mobilization, nor does it use sustained low-load force, which describes myofascial release.
  86. The correct answer is D (Compression of trapped air against the skin surface as the cupped hand strikes): the hollowed hand traps a pocket of air that compresses on contact, producing the characteristic popping sound. Shearing across fibers describes friction, not tapotement. Sustained traction describes a mobilization technique, not a percussive stroke, and forearm-transmitted vibration describes a distinct technique with continuous rather than percussive contact.
  87. The correct answer is C (It delivers a firmer, more forceful percussive contact than hacking): the closed-fist contact of beating distributes more force over a broader area, making it suitable for well-muscled or fleshy regions that tolerate deeper percussion. It is not lighter than hacking, it is not a sustained trigger point technique, and it remains percussive rather than a gliding compression stroke.
  88. The correct answer is A (Loose superficial skin such as over the face or scar tissue): pincement lifts small folds of skin and superficial fascia, making it suited to thinner, looser tissue rather than thick fascial bands or deep muscle. It is not directed at deep trigger points, and it is unrelated to joint capsule mobilization, which uses graded passive oscillation instead.
  89. The correct answer is D (Superficial fascia and cutaneous circulation near the skin surface): superficial effleurage is applied with light pressure and mainly stimulates skin and superficial fascia along with cutaneous blood flow. Deep venous and lymphatic effects require firmer, deeper effleurage instead. Joint capsules and tendon attachments are not the target of any effleurage stroke, superficial or deep.
  90. The correct answer is B (Localized adhesions and tension within deeper muscle layers): petrissage lifts, rolls, and squeezes muscle tissue, which makes it effective for working deeper adhesions and chronic tension that superficial strokes cannot reach. It is not primarily a relaxation opener, it does not target epidermal-only adhesions, and acute inflammation is generally a contraindication for vigorous kneading.
  91. The correct answer is D (The mobility and adherence of superficial fascia to underlying tissue): skin rolling lifts the skin away from deeper structures, revealing where superficial fascia is restricted or bound down and mobilizing those restrictions. It does not assess joint range of motion, which requires goniometric movement testing, and it is unrelated to resisted strength testing or heat penetration depth.
  92. The correct answer is D (A shearing and stretching force across the transverse width of the tissue): wringing moves the hands in opposite directions across the muscle, creating a twisting shear and stretch through its width. It is not percussive like tapotement, not a static sustained hold like ischemic compression, and it does not follow a linear venous-return path the way effleurage does.
  93. The correct answer is B (Circular friction moves in a rotary pattern rather than across fiber direction): circular friction works tissue in small rotary movements over a fixed point, whereas transverse friction moves specifically perpendicular to fiber orientation. Circular friction is applied to soft tissue generally, not exclusively bone, it involves no thrust component, and it can be performed manually without any mechanical device.
  94. The correct answer is A (To produce a traumatic hyperemia that mobilizes and remodels the fibers): moving perpendicular to the fiber direction creates a localized therapeutic hyperemia and mechanical broadening that helps mobilize fibers and encourage organized collagen remodeling. It is not intended to lengthen the whole muscle belly, it is localized rather than generalized to the limb, and it has no relationship to joint capsule mobilization technique.
  95. The correct answer is D (To concentrate mechanical force precisely on the specific lesion site): friction is deliberately localized and deep so its mechanical effect is concentrated exactly where an adhesion, scar, or fibrotic lesion is located rather than diffused broadly. It is not meant to spread evenly over an entire muscle group, it shares no relation to percussive tapotement rhythm, and it is a distinct technique from passive stretching used in remedial exercise.
  96. The correct answer is B (Fine vibration produces a smaller amplitude at a higher frequency): fine vibration is characterized by small, rapid trembling movements, whereas coarse vibration or shaking uses a larger amplitude at a comparatively lower frequency. Fine vibration is typically manual rather than requiring a mechanical device, and it maintains continuous contact rather than lifting off between beats, which would instead describe a percussive stroke.
  97. The correct answer is B (Promoting general relaxation and reducing overall muscle guarding): shaking or jostling rhythmically moves the tissue to encourage general relaxation and reduce protective muscle guarding. It is not intended to remodel dense adhered scar tissue, which needs friction, it does not restore joint accessory glide, which requires mobilization, and it does not produce the localized hyperemia associated with deep friction.
  98. The correct answer is B (Roughly ninety to one hundred twenty seconds or longer): myofascial release depends on sustaining gentle tension at the tissue barrier for an extended period, often ninety seconds or more, to allow viscoelastic creep and fascial release. A hold under five seconds is too brief for this mechanism, it has no relation to a single percussive stroke, and it is not tied to matching one breath cycle.
  99. The correct answer is A (It follows the tissue toward the path of greatest ease rather than resistance): indirect myofascial release guides the tissue along its path of least resistance, letting it unwind toward ease instead of pushing into the barrier. It is not a percussive tapping technique, it is not joint oscillation work, and it remains a manual hands-on technique rather than requiring mechanical vibration equipment.
  100. The correct answer is D (A softening or release of tension under the sustained pressure): the therapist maintains steady compression until the taut, tender point softens and releases beneath the fingers, at which point pressure can be adjusted or released.   101. The correct answer is B (To momentarily reduce pain perception, allowing a fuller passive stretch): the brief cooling sensation distracts from pain signals long enough for the muscle to be passively lengthened more fully than it would tolerate otherwise. It does not numb the tendon or block proprioception outright, it does not cause any thermal injury to the point, and its cooling effect is the opposite of a warming compress.
  102. The correct answer is D (A brief relaxation phase followed by a passive lengthening of the muscle): after the isometric hold, the muscle briefly relaxes due to neurological inhibition, and the therapist uses this window to passively take it into greater length.   103. The correct answer is B (The muscle being stretched itself, known as the agonist target): in contract-relax, the target muscle undergoing the stretch is the one that contracts isometrically before relaxing into a deeper stretch. The antagonist contracting instead describes a different PNF variant, an unrelated stabilizer is not the intended muscle, and simultaneous co-contraction is not part of this specific sequence.
  104. The correct answer is B (To use reciprocal inhibition to actively pull the limb into greater range): actively contracting the agonist reciprocally inhibits the just-relaxed antagonist, letting the client actively move further into the new range. It is not primarily meant to fatigue the antagonist, it has no connection to hydrotherapy sequencing, and it functions as a stretching technique rather than a formal strength assessment.
  105. The correct answer is A (Fifteen to thirty seconds per repetition, held without bouncing): static stretches are generally held steadily for about fifteen to thirty seconds to allow tissue elongation without triggering a protective stretch reflex. Two to three seconds is too brief for meaningful change, five minutes is excessive for a single hold, and tying duration strictly to one exhale is not a recognized clinical guideline.
  106.   107. Pure active range of motion has no outside assistance at all, fully passive movement involves no client effort whatsoever, and adding resistance throughout describes resisted exercise instead.
  108.   109. A large-amplitude movement to the limit of range describes a higher grade, a thrust beyond anatomical limit is outside a massage therapist's scope, and a sustained static hold is not how Maitland grades are defined.
  110. The correct answer is A (At the limit of available range, into the tissue resistance): grade IV mobilizations are small-amplitude oscillations performed right at the end of available range, working into the resistance to address stiffness. Applying at the very beginning or mid-range without resistance describes lower grades, and moving beyond the anatomical limit with a thrust describes a grade V manipulation, which falls outside this scope of technique.
  111.   112.   113.   114. The correct answer is B (Three minutes hot to one minute cold, repeated for several cycles): a common contrast bath protocol uses roughly three minutes of hot immersion followed by one minute of cold, cycled several times to pump circulation through alternating vasodilation and vasoconstriction.   115.   116. The correct answer is A (Cold, burning, aching, then numbness as the final sensation): the classic sequence of cold application sensations progresses from an initial cold feeling, through burning and aching, and finally to numbness or analgesia. The other answer choices reorder these stages incorrectly and do not reflect the sequence typically taught for cold application.
  117. The correct answer is A (Chronic stiffness in small distal joints such as the hands or feet): paraffin wax conforms well to small irregular joint surfaces and delivers gentle sustained heat suited to chronic stiffness in the hands and feet.   119. The remodeling and chronic stiffness stages, and fully matured scar tissue, are generally appropriate times to apply heat rather than times it is contraindicated.
  120.   126.   130.   132.   133.   145.   150.   159. The correct answer is C (Tendons, ligaments, and muscle at sites of localized lesions): Cyriax's original model specifically targeted tendon, ligament, and muscle lesions with cross-fiber friction to promote mobile scar formation. Superficial skin folds are the focus of pincement or skin rolling instead, joint capsules are addressed with mobilization technique, and bony prominences are generally areas to avoid direct friction pressure rather than target.
  160. The correct answer is A (To help maintain tissue length and reinforce treatment gains between visits): a home stretching program reinforces the gains made in treatment and helps maintain tissue length in the period between visits. It is not meant to replace ongoing clinical treatment altogether, it has no relationship to the localized hyperemia produced by friction technique, and end-feel assessment is a skilled clinical evaluation rather than a home exercise task.
  161. The correct answer is C (To introduce and conclude contact smoothly while assessing tissue tone): opening and closing effleurage smoothly transitions the client into and out of contact while allowing the therapist to assess general tissue tone and condition. It is not intended to create the hyperemia associated with friction, it does not remodel dense adhesions the way friction can, and it is a gliding stroke rather than a percussive one.
  162. The correct answer is B (Relaxation and lengthening techniques to reduce excessive tone): a hypertonic muscle has excessive resting tone, so treatment generally focuses on relaxation and lengthening approaches such as petrissage or myofascial release. Facilitation and strengthening techniques are instead used for a hypotonic muscle with reduced tone, tapotement applied to increase excitability is more suited to hypotonic tissue, and maximal resisted exercise is not the priority approach for reducing hypertonicity.
  163. The correct answer is A (To facilitate and increase neuromuscular activity in underactive tissue): tapotement's percussive stimulus can help facilitate and increase activity in a hypotonic muscle showing reduced tone. Reducing excessive tone through sustained low-load stretch instead describes myofascial release used for hypertonic tissue, breaking down adhesions describes a friction application, and numbing tissue before friction is unrelated to tapotement's stimulating purpose.
  164. The correct answer is C (To avoid triggering a protective stretch reflex and reduce injury risk): moving slowly and monitoring response helps avoid triggering the muscle spindle's protective stretch reflex, reducing the risk of straining tissue. This approach has no connection to tapotement's rhythm, it is not defined by matching myofascial release's specific hold time, and it bears no relationship to a contrast bath's hot-to-cold ratio.
  165. The correct answer is B (Both stimulate local circulation to prepare tissue for the modality): rhythmic compression and preparatory petrissage both work to stimulate local circulation, readying the tissue to respond well to the hydrotherapy modality that follows. Neither produces the specific hyperemia unique to deep friction, neither is a percussive technique, and neither functions as a sustained fascial-lengthening hold like myofascial release.
  166. The correct answer is C (From broad, lighter techniques toward more localized, deeper techniques): as treatment goals progress from general relaxation toward addressing a specific chronic adhesion, technique selection typically shifts from broader and lighter work toward more localized, deeper approaches like friction. The reverse direction does not match how treatment plans generally progress, and neither the resisted-to-passive nor hydrotherapy-to-tapotement patterns describe this typical progression.
  167. The correct answer is C (To gradually reintroduce movement safely as tolerance and control improve): starting with passive movement and progressing toward active movement allows tissue and neuromuscular control to be reintroduced safely as tolerance improves after immobilization. This progression is unrelated to producing friction's hyperemia, it does not replace ongoing joint assessment, and it bears no relationship to a contrast bath's hot-to-cold timing ratio.
  168. The correct answer is C (Vibration uses continuous small-amplitude oscillation rather than a static sustained hold): vibration delivers continuous oscillating movement to the tissue, while ischemic compression instead applies a static, unmoving sustained pressure until release is felt. Vibration can be performed manually without a mechanical device, it does not produce the hyperemia specifically associated with deep friction, and it is applied over muscle tissue generally rather than being restricted only to bony prominences.
  169. The correct answer is B (An open-ended question that invites a narrative description): Open-ended questions allow the client to describe symptoms in their own language, which surfaces details a closed question would miss. Closed and multiple choice questions restrict responses and can cause the therapist to overlook relevant history. Leading questions risk biasing the client toward an answer that fits the interviewer's assumption rather than the client's actual experience.
  170. The correct answer is C (The client's demonstrated capacity to understand and make the decision): Valid consent requires disclosure, voluntariness, and capacity, meaning the client understands the information and can reason about it. A signature alone does not establish understanding, and it is not sufficient on its own to prove capacity. Physician approval and an unrelated witness are not required elements of valid consent for massage therapy.
  171. The correct answer is B (The complete absence of pain at that moment): since 0 anchors the low end of the scale representing no pain. The value 10 represents the worst imaginable pain, not 0, and mild discomfort or fluctuating pain describe intermediate experiences rather than the scale's zero anchor. Anchoring both ends of the scale correctly is essential for tracking pain changes across treatments.
  172. The correct answer is D (The Subjective section): This section captures information reported directly by the client, including pain descriptions, location, and functional complaints that cannot be independently measured. The Objective section instead holds measurable findings such as range of motion and palpation results. The Assessment section interprets findings and the Plan section outlines future treatment, so neither is the place for the client's self-reported symptoms.
  173. The sternum midline and C7 spinous process are landmarks used in anterior and posterior views, not the lateral plumb line assessment.
  174. The correct answer is D (A trigger point taut band): as this is the classic palpable finding within myofascial tissue associated with referred pain on compression. A varicosity describes a dilated superficial vein and has no relationship to referred muscular pain patterns.
  175. The correct answer is B (Active testing is performed entirely by the client's own muscular effort): which contrasts with passive motion where the therapist moves the limb while the client relaxes. Active testing does not involve therapist resistance throughout the movement, since that describes resisted testing instead. Passive testing evaluates available joint range and tissue end feel, not muscular strength, so it cannot substitute for a strength assessment.
  176. The correct answer is A (Resisted range of motion testing): This technique isolates contractile tissue by having the client contract a muscle against therapist applied resistance while the joint stays relatively still. Passive testing instead moves the joint without client effort, and active testing has the client move unaided without added resistance. Neurodynamic tension testing evaluates nerve mobility through specific limb positioning rather than isolated muscle contraction against resistance.
  177. Biceps tendon pathology and thoracic outlet compression each have their own dedicated special tests distinct from this maneuver.

  179. The correct answer is D (Carpal tunnel syndrome affecting the median nerve): Tapping over the median nerve at the volar wrist reproduces tingling into the thumb, index, middle, and part of the ring finger when the nerve is irritated within the carpal tunnel. Ligament sprains and cartilage complex injuries are evaluated with stress and compression tests, not a percussion sign.
  180. The correct answer is A (Carpal tunnel syndrome of the median nerve): since sustained wrist flexion narrows the carpal tunnel and compresses the median nerve, reproducing its characteristic sensory distribution symptoms. Cubital tunnel and Guyon's canal involve the ulnar nerve, which is assessed with elbow flexion or wrist extension provocation rather than this specific flexion test.
  181. Hamstring length is more directly measured using an active knee extension or straight leg raise procedure.

Mid stance and pre-swing occur later in the cycle, after weight has already been accepted onto the reference limb.
  183. Acute pain describes symptoms present for days to a few weeks following an injury. Subacute pain falls in the intermediate range, and referred pain describes the pain's location relative to its source rather than its duration.

  190. Following the least to most invasive order keeps the assessment systematic and safe for the client.
